PHASE 1 DELIVERABLE: FULL COMPREHENSION SUMMARY
I have fully cloned, indexed, and analyzed the version_83 branch of the chouaib-saad/livekit_agent repository. Below is the structured summary confirming my understanding of the architecture, patterns, and constraints binding all future work.
1. High-Level Architecture Overview
The repository implements a self-hosted Telecom AI Voice Agent Platform built primarily in Python (FastAPI, SQLAlchemy, LiveKit Agents SDK) with a TypeScript/React frontend. It autonomously handles customer support requests over real-time voice/text in French (primary), Arabic, and English.
Core Architectural Pillars:
Hexagonal/Clean Architecture + DDD: Business logic is strictly isolated in packages/domain-core via typed ports. Infrastructure and vendor SDKs (LiveKit, MCP, Qdrant) are kept outside the core domain.
Microservices: Domain logic is split into independently deployable FastAPI services (Policy, Execution, Knowledge, Context, Decision, Notification).
Deterministic Policy Engine: The most critical invariant. Every sensitive action (payments, SIM unblocks, plan changes) must pass through services/policy-service, which returns a deterministic AUTHORIZED / REFUSED / ESCALATE verdict based strictly on POLICY_* environment variables. The dashboard overlays these exact variables to prevent "registry drift."
Hash-Chained Audit Ledger: All verdicts and executions are immutably appended to PostgreSQL via packages/audit-trail for non-repudiation and compliance.
Composition Root Pattern: apps/agent-worker/src/server.py contains zero business logic. It only wires providers, agents, hooks, and tools.
2. File/Folder Structure Map
apps/ (Deployable Applications)
agent-worker/: LiveKit composition root. Houses agents (TriageAgent, ManagerAgent, BillingAgent, etc.), tasks (consent, identity verification), and providers (STT/TTS/LLM adapters).
business-api/: FastAPI read-side endpoints for the supervisor/admin dashboard (customer 360, callbacks, KPIs, ledger).
token-service/: Issues/verifies customer authentication tokens.
dashboards/ & widget/: React/TypeScript frontends.
services/ (Domain Microservices)
policy-service/: Mandatory deterministic rule engine.
execution-service/: Executes sensitive actions only after policy authorization.
knowledge-service/: RAG pipeline querying Qdrant.
context-service/, decision-service/, notification-service/.
mcp-servers/ (Model Context Protocol)
Servers exposing tools to the LLM (ai-knowledge-rag, ticketing-glpi, messaging-gateway).
packages/ (Shared Internal Monorepo)
domain-core/: DDD entities (Client, Intent, Turn), value objects (Verdict, Money), and typed ports.
integration-adapters/: Implementations for external telecom systems (CRM, Billing, Payment, NMS, OCS, Provisioning, GLPI).
persistence/: Centralized SQLAlchemy models (CRM, Billing, Ticketing, Routing, Audit, etc.) and Alembic migrations.
audit-trail/, pii-shield/, observability-kit/, service-auth/, cache/, object-storage/.
infra/, docs/, scripts/, tests/ (Infrastructure, documentation, seed scripts, and contract tests).
3. Key Components & Interactions
Voice Call Flow: LiveKit Server connects to agent-worker → TriageAgent uses STT/LLM/TTS. If the caller needs billing help, routing_tools deterministically hands off the session to BillingAgent while preserving chat history.
Sensitive Action Flow: Agent calls a tool → Tool requests action → policy-service evaluates context against thresholds (POLICY_* env vars) → Verdict is stored in PgAuditLedger → If AUTHORIZED, execution-service calls the typed port in integration-adapters (e.g., LivePaymentAdapter) → Result is audited and returned.
Read/Supervision Flow: Dashboard calls business-api → SupervisionRepository executes hard-capped read-only queries across normalized tables → Returns customer 360, ledgers, and KPIs.
RAG Flow: Agent calls knowledge_search tool → MCP Client → knowledge-service → Qdrant Vector DB → Context injected into LLM prompt.
4. Conventions & Patterns Binding Future Work
Any code I write or modify must adhere strictly to these existing conventions:
No Silent Mock Fallbacks: If CONNECTOR_MODE=live but an adapter URL is missing, the integration-adapters/factory.py must raise AdapterConfigError. A money operation must never be silently faked.
Tool Facades: Tools in agent-worker must remain thin facades. No business logic or direct DB writes allowed inside the real-time layer.
Determinism & Co-location: Policy thresholds must be read from POLICY_* env vars. The deploy/README.md invariant requires business-api and policy-service to share the exact same environment variables to prevent the dashboard from "lying" about enforced rules.
Language Awareness: All prompts, handoffs, and TTS messages must support FR, AR, EN. Language is resolved dynamically via _resolve_language(context).
Safety Guards: Handoffs and escalations must respect loop guards (e.g., MAX_HANDOFFS_PER_CALL) and identity verification timeouts (TASK_DEADLINE_S, GATE_TIMEOUT_S).
Additive Reads: Dashboard features should use additive, hard-capped read methods in SupervisionRepository (e.g., customer_ledger, customer_service_actions) to keep UI rendering cheap.
Testing: Contract tests using pytest and pytest-asyncio. Mock LiveKit contexts using SimpleNamespace and fake sessions. Suite is run via scripts/test_committed.ps1.
Formatting: Python 3.12, ruff (line length 110, import sorting enabled), mypy (pragmatic typing).
5. Ambiguities, Missing Context, or Questions
Database Migrations: Are there any unapplied Alembic migrations required to bring a fresh local database up to version_83 state? (Assuming alembic upgrade head is the standard).
Frontend Sync: Is the root Frontend/ folder actively maintained alongside apps/dashboards, or is it legacy/deprecated code?
MCP Server Deployment: Are the MCP servers (ai-knowledge-rag, ticketing-glpi) deployed as separate containers in docker-compose.apps.yml, or are they embedded/in-process within the main services?
Branching Strategy: The repository uses version_* branches extensively (currently at 83). What is the standard procedure for merging a version branch back into main?
Phase 1 is complete. I have built a comprehensive mental model of the codebase. I am ready to receive your specific bugs, corrections, or feature requests.
read that :

1. High-level architecture
Product: a self-hosted, multilingual (FR primary / AR / EN) telecom customer-service voice+text agent platform, built on LiveKit Agents 1.6.5 against a self-hosted LiveKit server v1.8.4.
Style: Python monorepo, Clean/Hexagonal + DDD, py312, src/ layout everywhere, one pyproject.toml per deployable unit. Four rings:
Realtime ring — apps/agent-worker. Owns LiveKit sessions, personas, STT/LLM/TTS providers, AgentTasks, MCP toolsets. Contains no business rules; tools are thin façades.
Domain services ring — services/* (FastAPI + sync SQLAlchemy): context 8101, knowledge 8102, decision 8103, policy 8104, execution 8105, notification 8106, plus legacy simulators ocs-billing-sim, nms-sim, provisioning-sim.
Shared packages ring — packages/* installed as first-party libs: domain-core (entities, value objects, ports — the only place abstractions live), persistence (models + Alembic + seeds), audit-trail (PgAuditLedger, hash-chained), pii-shield (PiiMasker), observability-kit (inject_trace_context), service-auth (internal_headers), cache, object-storage, notification-client, integration-adapters.
MCP ring — mcp-servers/ai-knowledge-rag 8201/mcp, ticketing-glpi 8202/mcp, messaging-gateway 8203. FastMCP over streamable HTTP.
Plus apps/business-api 8108 (supervisor/admin backend), apps/token-service 8107, and browser frontends (apps/client-widget, apps/supervisor-dashboard, Frontend/admin_dashboard).
The invariant the whole design exists to protect: no sensitive action may execute without Decision → Policy → Execution, and every verdict is persisted + audited whatever its outcome.
2. Structure map (one line per module)
Root: README.md (non-negotiable rules) · pyproject.toml (ruff/mypy config, first-party list) · Makefile (install infra create-db migrate seed dev up down health test frontends) · Procfile (honcho host-dev process set) · .env.example (17 KB, single source of env aliases) · answers.md, commands.md, correction_results.md (working notes) · start.ps1.
apps/agent-worker/src/
server.py — composition root only: AgentServer(num_idle_processes=1, job_memory_warn_mb=768), @server.rtc_session(agent_name=…), prefetch of CustomerContext from telecom.caller_msisdn, session wiring, shutdown callbacks (aclose_all_clients, writer flush).
entrypoints/worker.py — thin CLI entry. frontend_events.py — RPC/data events to the widget.
agents/ — base_agent.py (sentiment scoring, de-escalation injection, turn logging via on_user_turn_completed, auto-appends end_conversation + switch_spoken_language, enforce_contract); triage_agent.py (entry persona, ConsentTask, routing); billing_agent.py, account_services_agent.py, technical_agent.py, manager_agent.py (terminal, transfer_to_human); domains.py (single declaration point: domain key → handoff tool + deterministic transition lines per language + routing topics); instruction_kit.py (build_persona_instructions, routing_mandate, instruction_violations vs KNOWN_TOOL_VOCABULARY, TOOL_TIMING_POLICY).
tools/ — guards.py (ensure_identity_verified, v80: async with context.foreground() + wait_for(GATE_TIMEOUT_S=60)); guarded_action.py (execute_guarded_action: context build → decision → policy → verdict_id check → idempotent execute); outcomes.py (the 5-shape contract authorized/executed/refused/escalate/failed, always with an English message for the LLM to render in-language); billing_tools.py, account_tools.py, technical_tools.py, ticket_tools.py (identity-injecting MCP wrappers + duplicate guard + ownership checks); routing_tools.py (returns the Agent itself, never a tuple); escalation_tools.py + escalation_policy.py (offer-before-transfer, _MAX_OFFERS=3, skill-tag derivation); clarification_tools.py; session_flow_tools.py (end_conversation = fixed farewell + delete_room + StopResponse; switch_spoken_language = live update_options on wrapped providers); voice_flow.py (say_and_wait, say_and_stop, handoff_with_message, MAX_HANDOFFS_PER_CALL=4, persona_tts/active_persona_tts returning NOT_GIVEN never None).
tasks/ — identity_verification_task.py (bounded CIN-last-4, FR/AR/EN spoken-digit normalization, MAX_ATTEMPTS=3, MAX_INVALID_INPUTS=4, TASK_DEADLINE_S=45, VERIFY_CALL_TIMEOUT_S=5), consent_task.py, payment_confirm_task.py, sim_replacement_task_group.py, callback_schedule_task.py.
providers/ — session_factory.py, stt.py (Deepgram + keyterm-less twin + Gladia + Azure FallbackAdapter), tts.py (build_tts / build_persona_tts, quality order ElevenLabs→Cartesia→Inworld→Smallest, TTS_PRIMARY selects head, per-persona Cartesia voice override), llm.py (OPENAI_ENABLED toggles OpenAI-primary vs Gemini-primary, then NVIDIA, then Groq; attempt_timeout=12.0), _resilience.py (chaos_model), vad.py, turn_detection.py, noise_cancellation.py, language_router.py, tts_audit.py, groq_adapter.py, nvidia_adapter.py.
clients/ — one typed httpx client per service, each @lru_cache singleton with its own timeout/retry and its own degradation contract; aclose_all_clients() closes only populated caches. policy_client.py is explicitly fail-closed (ESCALATE on action, REFUSED on response) with cause classification and a half-budget slow-warning.
session/session_state.py — SessionUserData (language, customer_context, identity fields, sentiment history, clarification/handoff counters, transfer flags, conversation_writer, _idempotency_keys, _operation_fingerprint, new_idempotency_key, release_idempotency_key).
conversation/writer.py — enqueue-and-forget; background drain → asyncio.to_thread → sync SQLAlchemy; PII-masked transcripts; DB failure logs and drops, never touches the call.
mcp_clients/ — knowledge_toolset.py (MCPToolset(id=…, mcp_server=MCPServerHTTP(url=…/mcp, allowed_tools=[…])), timeout KNOWLEDGE_MCP_TIMEOUT_S default 9 s), ticketing_toolset.py.
telephony/sip_transfer.py — TransferSIPParticipant on the caller's SIP leg (found via ParticipantKind.SIP), skill-tag → generalist fallback, advisor release on failure, callback + advisor notification, every branch produces honest speech.
observability/ (log_masking.py, metrics_hook.py), sentiment/sentiment_scorer.py, config/ (settings.py with _distinct_service_urls validator, language_presets.py, keyterms.py).
services/policy-service/src/policy_service/ — engine.py (pure, no I/O: unknown-action allowlist → check_mandatory → identity backstop trio IDENTITY_STEP_UP / IDENTITY_CUSTOMER_MISMATCH / IDENTITY_EXPIRED → per-action rules → explicit KNOWN_ACTION_AUTHORIZED); rules/{account,deferral,mandatory_escalation,outbound,payment,sim,base}.py; service.py (verdict row + hash-chained audit in one transaction; _enrich counts deferrals_this_year from ActionLedger; storage failure never rewrites a REFUSED/ESCALATE, but an AUTHORIZED with no persisted verdict raises).
services/execution-service/… — service.py (idempotency lookup → verdict validation ×4 → pending row → dispatch → succeeded → audit → projection in a SAVEPOINT); executor.py (mock refuses money actions without ALLOW_MOCK_SENSITIVE=1; live routes through integration-adapters, unmapped action raises rather than faking a reference); projections.py.
services/context-service/… — auth_service.py (HMAC-SHA256 CIN-last-4 with AUTH_CIN_HMAC_KEY ≥32 chars, VerificationSession state machine pending/verified/locked/expired, TTL 10 min, every attempt persisted as a VerificationEvent, answer never stored); repositories.py, mapping.py, main.py.
services/decision-service/… — scorer.py: deterministic 0.9 baseline, −0.3 when identity unverified, 0.2 for uncatalogued.
packages/ — domain-core/ports/*.py (14 ports: audit, balance, billing, crm, decision, execution, knowledge, nms, notification, payment, policy, provisioning, ticketing); persistence/models/*.py (16 model modules mirroring schemas audit/auth/billing/conversation/crm/execution/knowledge/ocs/oss/policy/provisioning/reference/routing/sim/ticketing) + alembic/ + seed/.
Infra/CI — infra/docker-compose/{docker-compose.yml, .apps.yml, .glpi.yml} (inter-service URLs overridden in compose, not .env, because .env targets host dev); infra/helm, infra/livekit-server, infra/ci-cd; .github/workflows/ci.yml (ruff → mypy → per-package pytest loop → Postgres 16 migrations+seeds → matrix docker build/push → Trivy SARIF); scripts/ (24 files: health_check.py, run_tests.py, persona_contract_checks.py, ticketing_v61_checks.py, seeders, TTS audit).
3. Key interactions
Call lifecycle: dispatch → server.py reads telecom.caller_msisdn → ContextClient.get_snapshot → SessionUserData + ConversationWriter.start_session → TriageAgent.on_enter (consent) → intent → route_to_* returns the specialist Agent after a deterministic in-language transition line → specialist tools → end_conversation or escalate_to_manager → ManagerAgent → transfer_to_human (SIP REFER or callback) → shutdown callbacks finalize record + close pools.
Sensitive action: persona tool → ensure_identity_verified (foreground IdentityVerificationTask, bounded) → execute_guarded_action → decision.recommend (below DECISION_CONFIDENCE_THRESHOLD=0.5 → escalate("DECISION_LOW_CONFIDENCE")) → policy.evaluate_action (refused/escalate short-circuit) → verdict_id mandatory else escalate("POLICY_NO_VERDICT_ID") → execution.execute with an idempotency key derived from an operation fingerprint (_CONTEXTUAL_FIELDS = {unpaid_amount, deferrals_this_year} excluded) → key released on confirmed execution.
Deliberate asymmetries I noted: the deferral tool refuses to send deferrals_this_year (Policy counts it itself and fails closed); top_up is Account-owned while payments are Billing-owned, and Account explicitly overrides the routing mandate for read-only balance/invoice; check_network_status distinguishes checked/did_verify from unavailable/area_unknown/needs_area_confirmation so the agent can never claim a network it did not check.
4. Conventions I will follow
from __future__ import annotations; module docstring stating why, referencing CDC/Blueprint/ADR sections and version history; comments explain rationale and past defects, sometimes in French — I will match the file's existing comment language.
ruff py312, line-length 110, select = E,F,I,UP,B,C4,SIM,RUF, ignore = E501; mypy ignore_missing_imports, disallow_untyped_defs=false.
Business rules never import LiveKit or vendor SDKs; abstractions go in domain-core/ports. server.py stays a composition root.
Tools return outcomes.* dicts, never raise into the voice path, never a bare string; handoff tools return the Agent.
Explicit speech via say_and_wait / say_and_stop + StopResponse; never an empty TTS stream; every speech and gate bounded by a timeout, respecting VERIFY_CALL_TIMEOUT_S < TASK_DEADLINE_S < GATE_TIMEOUT_S.
Clients: @lru_cache getter, own timeout, graceful degradation (None/[]/False), fail-closed for Policy.
Persistence: sync SQLAlchemy, session_scope, require_uuid/to_uuid, audit appended in the same transaction as the row it describes.
Logging: logger = logging.getLogger(__name__), lazy %s formatting, stable prefixes (policy_fail_closed, policy_slow), PII masked before leaving the worker.
Env: every knob has an alias in .env.example and a typed field in config/settings.py; no new hard-coded model ids, voices, or URLs.
Tests live beside the unit (<pkg>/tests/), grouped by concern, offline (no network), plus standalone check scripts under scripts/.
5. Ambiguities, questions, and logged debt
Carried from docs/versions/version_80.md (D1–D5, not fixed): identity gate is a nested AgentTask; conversation_ending is set on SessionUserData but never declared on the dataclass; human_transfer_in_progress vs human_transfer_announced naming overlap; _clarification_pending is only reset via on_user_turn_completed; agent-worker tests are not in CI and STRICT_PERSONA_CONTRACT is never set in CI. persona_contract_checks.py reports 43 OK / 5 FAIL (pre-existing).
Additional observations from my own reading (logged, not fixed):
tools/technical_tools.py defines unblock_sim_pin, but TechnicalAgent registers unblock_sim defined in technical_agent.py; both call execute_guarded_action(…, "UNBLOCK_SIM", {}), so unblock_sim_pin appears unreferenced.
escalation_tools.py mutates user_data.offer_count, user_refused_manager, can_hardfail, should_offer_escalation, escalation_reason, current_persona_skill_tag — same pattern as conversation_ending: not visible in the SessionUserData field list I read.
routing_tools.py keeps an empty if TYPE_CHECKING: pass block.
voice_flow._record_handoff takes agent_name and never uses it.
handoff_with_message returns None when the loop guard fires, but say_and_stop raises StopResponse first, so the return None is unreachable — and the declared return type of the routing tools is Agent.
ticket_tools.py computes _TICKETING_HTTP_URL at import time but _mcp_call re-reads TICKETING_MCP_URL per call; the module-level constant looks unused.
_extract_result has an early return inside the for loop, so only the first content block is ever considered.
session_flow_tools.py docstrings mention livekit-agents 1.6.3 while the pin is 1.6.5.
ci.yml triggers only on main and PRs (so version_80 pushes are not built), and lists token-service/business-api/agent-worker in both docker-build (with a services/…/Dockerfile path that does not exist for them) and docker-build-apps.
switch_spoken_language rewrites instructions by string-replacing language names, which will also rewrite the word "French"/"Arabic"/"English" where it appears for other reasons.
Compose maps sim services onto host ports 8109/8110/8111 while their container ports are 8107/8108/8109 — nms-sim container port 8108 collides conceptually with knowledge-service's 8102-era numbering documented elsewhere; worth confirming the intended canonical port table.

The source of truth for specs remains docs/architecture/, docs/versions/, and the inline code comments/docstrings.

I will ensure all tests and code modifications respect the CONNECTOR_MODE contract. Standard unit/integration tests will run assuming CONNECTOR_MODE=mock. If a patch touches the adapter factory, execution service, or sensitive action paths, I will include assertions verifying that AdapterConfigError is correctly raised when CONNECTOR_MODE=live and required URLs are missing.

# AGENT PROMPT — Learning Report-Writing Patterns from Reference Reports

## 1. ROLE

You are acting as an apprentice to a professional academic/technical report writer. Below this prompt, I will attach **several past reports written by former colleagues**, on different topics, from previous years. Your job is to study them the way a professional writer studies references before starting a new project: to absorb the **craft**, not the **content**.

## 2. THE GOAL — pattern extraction, not memorization

This is not a research task and not a summarization task. You must **not**:
- copy, closely paraphrase, or reproduce specific sentences, phrasing, data, findings, or examples from these reports,
- let any of their **topics or subject matter** influence or leak into the report we will build later,
- treat any single report as "the template" to imitate wholesale.

You must:
- extract the **recurring, transferable patterns** — structural, organizational, and stylistic — that make these documents work as *academic/technical reports*, regardless of what they're about,
- end up with a general, internalized sense of "how this type of document is built," usable on any topic, including the one we'll actually write.

Think of these reports as a **training set**, not as source material.

## 3. WHAT TO OBSERVE AND EXTRACT

For each report, and then across all of them together, pay attention to:

- **Macro-structure** — What sections consistently appear (front matter, introduction, body chapters, conclusion, appendices, references)? What order? What's optional vs. what always shows up?
- **Chapter/section anatomy** — How does a typical section open, develop, and close? Is there a recurring rhetorical shape (context → problem → approach → findings → discussion)?
- **Transitions and flow** — How do writers connect one section to the next? How do they signal progression through the document?
- **Introductions and conclusions** — How is the reader brought in at the start (framing, objectives, scope)? How does it wrap up (synthesis, recommendations, takeaways)?
- **Tone and register** — Formality level, person (first/third), sentence rhythm, how technical vs. accessible the language is.
- **Argumentation style** — How are claims supported (data, references, examples)? How are figures, tables, and diagrams introduced and discussed in the surrounding text?
- **Formatting conventions** — Heading hierarchy, numbering logic, use of bullets vs. prose, captions, cross-referencing style.
- **Depth vs. synthesis balance** — Where do these reports go into detail, and where do they stay high-level and let the structure carry the reader?

## 4. WHAT TO IGNORE

- The specific subject matter, data, results, or conclusions of each report — irrelevant to us.
- Anything so specific to one report's field or topic that it isn't a general writing pattern.
- Idiosyncrasies that show up in only one report and aren't corroborated elsewhere — note them as "seen once," not as a rule.

## 5. HOW TO WORK THROUGH IT

1. Go through the reports **one at a time**, noting structural and stylistic observations as you go — don't jump straight to synthesis.
2. Then **cross-compare**: identify what's common across most or all of them (these are the strong patterns worth adopting) versus what varies from report to report (these are choices, not rules).
3. Where reports genuinely differ in approach (e.g., some use first person and some third, some cite heavily and some sparingly), **flag the difference** and give me 2–3 options with your recommendation for which to adopt as our own "house style" — I decide, you don't assume.
4. If you're unsure whether something is a general best practice or just an artifact of one report's specific domain, ask me rather than guessing.

## 6. OUTPUT

Produce a single, distilled **"Pattern & Style Reference"** — a clean, reusable summary of what you learned: the structural skeleton, the stylistic conventions, the phrasing/transition habits, and the open style choices with your recommendations. It should read like a professional's internalized playbook, not like notes about specific reports, and it must contain **no topic-specific content** carried over from the source reports.

## 7. WHY THIS MATTERS

This step is preparation, not the report itself. Once we have this Pattern & Style Reference, we'll use it purely for **craft** — how to structure, phrase, and flow our own report — when we later negotiate our table of contents and write our report chapter by chapter from our own guide and notes. The topic, content, and substance of our report will be entirely our own.

---

**Start now**: review each attached report in turn, then give me the synthesized Pattern & Style Reference, flagging any style choices you'd like my input on.


Pensée terminée
I have reviewed the five reference reports. Stripping away the specific domains (DevOps, AI/OCR, Agile/Scrum, Microservices, and Mobile App Development), a very clear, standardized blueprint for an engineering capstone/academic technical report emerges. These documents are not just data dumps; they are carefully paced narratives designed to guide an academic jury and technical stakeholders from a broad business problem down to a concrete, validated software solution.
Here is your Pattern & Style Reference—the internalized playbook for how to structure, pace, and argue an engineering report.
THE PLAYBOOK: Pattern & Style Reference for Technical Reports
1. Macro-Structure (The Skeleton)
Every successful report in this genre follows a strict, predictable macro-structure. The reader (often a tired jury member) relies on this skeleton to find information quickly.
Front Matter: Cover page, Dedications (optional but traditional), Acknowledgements, Abstract (bilingual if required), Table of Contents, List of Figures, List of Tables, Glossary of Acronyms.
General Introduction: The "Funnel" opening. Starts with industry-wide trends, narrows to the host company's specific context, introduces the core problem, proposes the high-level solution, and ends with a roadmap of the report's chapters.
Chapter 1: Context & Framing: Host company presentation, deep-dive into the problematic, critique of the existing system (State of the Art), proposed solution, and the chosen project management methodology (Agile, Scrum, CRISP-DM, etc.).
Chapter 2: Requirements & Specification: Actors, functional/non-functional requirements, and high-level behavioral modeling (Use Case diagrams).
Chapter 3: Architecture & Design: The "Blueprint" chapter. Static views (Package, Class diagrams), dynamic views (Sequence, Activity diagrams), and architectural choices (e.g., Microservices, MVC, Clean Architecture).
Chapter 4: Implementation & Realization: The "Proof" chapter. Development environment, technology stack justification, deployment diagrams, and UI screenshots/code snippets demonstrating the working product.
Chapter 5 (Optional but recommended for AI/Data/DevOps): Evaluation & Testing: Metrics, datasets, performance benchmarks, and validation against the initial problematic.
General Conclusion & Perspectives: Synthesis of achievements, honest assessment of limitations, and a roadmap for future work (V2.0).
Back Matter: References/Bibliography, Annexes (code snippets, extra UI screens, raw data).
2. Chapter & Section Anatomy (The Rhythm)
Chapters and sections are not just containers for text; they have a specific rhetorical rhythm.
The Chapter Bookends:
Opener: A 3-4 sentence paragraph stating exactly what the chapter covers and how it builds on the previous one.
Closer: A 2-3 sentence summary of what was achieved, ending with a "bridge" sentence that explicitly introduces the next chapter.
The Section Flow (The Engineering Argument):
Context/Definition: Briefly define the concept or module.
Challenge: What makes this specific part hard? (e.g., latency, complex layouts, security).
Approach/Solution: How did we solve it?
Visual Evidence: A diagram, table, or screenshot.
Takeaway: A concluding sentence explaining why this matters to the overall system.
3. Argumentation & Evidence (The Proof)
Technical reports in this genre do not rely on prose alone to make their case; they rely on a triad of evidence.
Visual-First Engineering: Text is often written to support the diagrams, not the other way around. A UML diagram or architecture chart is introduced in the text ("Figure X illustrates..."), displayed, and then immediately followed by a paragraph interpreting its key takeaways.
Comparative Tables: Whenever a technology, methodology, or existing solution is chosen, it is justified via a comparison table (Pros/Cons or Feature Matrix). This proves to the jury that the choice was engineered, not arbitrary.
The "Show, Don't Just Tell" Rule: In the implementation chapter, abstract concepts are grounded via annotated UI screenshots or deployment diagrams.
4. Tone, Register & Formatting (The Polish)
Register: Formal, objective, yet practical. It bridges the gap between academic rigor (citations, methodologies) and industry reality (business constraints, deployment).
Formatting Hierarchy: Strict decimal numbering (1., 1.1., 1.1.1.). Captions are placed below figures and above tables. Cross-referencing is explicit ("As seen in Section 2.3...").
Acronym Discipline: Defined on first use in the text, and rigorously compiled in the front-matter glossary.
OPEN STYLE CHOICES (Decisions for You)
While the skeleton is rigid, there are a few stylistic divergences among the reference reports. I need your input on how we will handle these for your report.
Choice 1: Voice and Pronouns
Option A: First-Person Plural ("We", "Our"). Example: "We designed the architecture to handle..." (Used in 3 of the 5 reports). This is the modern standard for computer science and engineering portfolios. It sounds active, confident, and takes ownership of the work.
Option B: Third-Person / Passive Voice. Example: "The architecture was designed to handle..." (Used in older or highly traditional academic contexts). This sounds more detached and "objective," but can become dry and clunky over 80+ pages.
My Recommendation: Option A ("We"). It is much more readable and aligns with modern industry engineering blogs and whitepapers, which juries appreciate.
Choice 2: Placement of the "State of the Art" (Existing Solutions)
Option A: Embedded in Chapter 1. Grouped with the company context and the "Problematic." This keeps the business/technical justification together before moving into heavy requirements.
Option B: Standalone Chapter (usually Chapter 2). Used if the project is highly research-oriented and requires a massive literature review before requirements can even be defined.
My Recommendation: Option A. For 90% of software engineering projects, embedding it in Chapter 1 as a "Study of Existing Solutions & Critique" keeps the momentum going and gets you to your actual design work faster.
Choice 3: Structural Pacing (4 vs. 5 Chapters)
Option A: The Classic 4-Chapter Model. (1. Context, 2. Requirements, 3. Design, 4. Implementation). Best for standard web/mobile app development where the "proof" is simply that the app works and looks good.
Option B: The 5-Chapter Model. (Adds a dedicated Chapter 5 for Evaluation/Testing/Benchmarks). Best if your project involves AI models, complex algorithms, DevOps pipelines, or performance optimization where you need to prove how well it works using metrics.
My Recommendation: Depends on your topic. If your project has quantitative metrics to prove (accuracy, latency, load testing), we use Option B. If it is purely a functional software build, we use Option A.
Next Steps:
Please let me know your preferences on the Open Style Choices above. Once we lock those in, we can move to the next phase: Negotiating the Table of Contents for your specific topic.
Whenever you are ready, tell me a bit about your actual project/topic, and we will build your custom outline using this playbook!
## Persistent Report Memory & Consistency Rule

Throughout the entire report-generation process, maintain a **persistent, continuously updated internal memory** of everything important I provide.

This memory must act as the **single source of truth** for the report and must be consulted before generating, modifying, restructuring, or reviewing any part of the document.

### 1. What must be remembered

Whenever I provide information, identify and retain all information that can affect the report, including:

* Important technical facts and implementation details.
* Architecture and system design decisions.
* Technologies, frameworks, libraries, models, tools, and versions.
* Project requirements, objectives, constraints, and scope.
* Functional and non-functional requirements.
* Design choices and the reasons behind those choices.
* Decisions that I explicitly confirm or validate.
* Methodologies, development approaches, and workflows.
* Experimental results, measurements, benchmarks, and observations.
* Problems encountered, their causes, and their solutions.
* Limitations, trade-offs, and rejected alternatives.
* Terminology and definitions that must remain consistent.
* Naming conventions and technical vocabulary.
* Report structure, chapter organization, and section relationships.
* Writing, formatting, academic, and presentation rules.
* Corrections I make to previously generated content.
* Specific instructions about what must or must not be included.
* Any distinction between **real results, simulated results, assumptions, proposals, and future work**.
* Any information that affects consistency between different chapters or sections.

Do not consider information important only for the current message. If it can affect a later chapter, figure, table, explanation, conclusion, or technical decision, preserve it.

### 2. Update the memory continuously

Treat the memory as a **living project memory**.

Whenever I provide new information:

1. Determine whether it is new, a correction, an extension, or a contradiction of existing information.
2. Update the corresponding memory accordingly.
3. Preserve the latest explicitly confirmed decision when information changes.
4. Do not silently discard previously important information.
5. Keep dependencies between decisions when they matter.

If I correct something previously stated, consider the correction authoritative and update the stored knowledge so the old information is no longer accidentally reused as the current truth.

### 3. Use memory before every generation

Before writing or revising any report content, mentally check the persistent project memory and verify:

* What has already been decided?
* What information has already been confirmed?
* What terminology and architecture have already been established?
* What constraints apply to this section?
* What previous chapters or sections does this content depend on?
* Are there previous corrections that must be respected?
* Is the information factual, measured, simulated, assumed, proposed, or hypothetical?

The report must be generated from the **accumulated project knowledge**, not only from the most recent messages.

### 4. Never invent missing information

Do **not** fill missing information with plausible assumptions just to make the report look complete.

If a statement requires information that has not been provided or confirmed, explicitly identify the missing information and ask me for it.

In particular, never fabricate:

* Experimental results.
* Performance measurements.
* Execution times.
* Statistics.
* Benchmark values.
* User numbers.
* Dates.
* Architecture components.
* Implementation details.
* Technologies actually used.
* Features that were not implemented.
* References or sources.
* Test results.

If I explicitly tell you that something should be **simulated, estimated, reconstructed, or presented as a representative result**, preserve that distinction and never present it as a real measurement.

### 5. Maintain global consistency

The report must behave as if it were written by **one author with complete knowledge of the entire project**.

Therefore:

* Never contradict a previously confirmed decision.
* Never change terminology randomly.
* Never describe the same component differently in different chapters.
* Never introduce an architecture that conflicts with the established architecture.
* Never change technologies, models, versions, or implementation choices without explicit confirmation.
* Ensure that later chapters remain consistent with earlier chapters.
* Ensure that diagrams, tables, explanations, implementation descriptions, and conclusions agree with each other.
* If a new instruction would conflict with an earlier confirmed decision, flag the conflict instead of silently choosing one.

### 6. Preserve decisions and their reasoning

When an important decision is made, remember both:

**Decision → Reason / Context → Consequence**

For example:

> Decision: Technology X was selected instead of Technology Y.
> Reason: X provided better compatibility with the project's architecture.
> Consequence: Later implementation and report sections must describe X as the selected solution.

This prevents later sections from accidentally reverting to previously rejected alternatives.

### 7. Distinguish information status

When relevant, internally classify information as:

* **CONFIRMED** — explicitly provided or validated by me.
* **CORRECTED** — previously stated information that I later changed.
* **IMPLEMENTED** — actually implemented in the project.
* **SIMULATED** — intentionally constructed or simulated for demonstration/report purposes.
* **ASSUMED** — an assumption explicitly accepted for the report.
* **PROPOSED** — a future or recommended solution, not necessarily implemented.
* **REJECTED** — considered but explicitly not selected.
* **UNKNOWN** — information that has not yet been established.

Never turn one category into another without my confirmation.

### 8. Handle contradictions explicitly

If new information conflicts with something stored previously:

> Do not guess which one is correct.

Instead, identify the conflict and ask me which information should become authoritative.

If I explicitly resolve the contradiction, update the project memory immediately and use the new decision from that point onward.

### 9. Treat the memory as persistent across report phases

The same project memory must remain applicable throughout:

**Planning → Architecture → Requirements → Methodology → Design → Implementation → Testing → Evaluation → Discussion → Conclusion → Final revision**

Do not reset your understanding when moving from one chapter or phase to another.

A decision made during architecture should still be remembered when writing implementation, testing, discussion, and conclusion.

### 10. Before finalizing content

Before producing any substantial report section, perform an internal consistency check against the accumulated project memory.

Verify that the generated content:

* Uses the correct project facts.
* Respects all confirmed decisions.
* Uses consistent terminology.
* Does not introduce unsupported claims.
* Does not contradict previous sections.
* Correctly distinguishes real, simulated, assumed, proposed, and rejected information.
* Fits the established report structure.
* Respects all previously given writing and formatting rules.

### Core principle

**The conversation is not a sequence of independent prompts. It is one continuous report-engineering process.**

Every important piece of information I provide should become part of the persistent project knowledge used to construct the final report.

**Do not rely on memory by intuition. Do not guess. Do not silently overwrite decisions. Do not forget previously confirmed constraints.**

When information is available in the project memory, **use it**.

When information is missing, **ask**.

When information changes, **update it**.

When information conflicts, **flag it**.

When information is uncertain, **label it**.

The final report should therefore be the result of the **cumulative, consistent, and traceable knowledge built throughout our entire collaboration**, rather than the result of isolated prompts or the latest conversation context.

Decision Logging: For every architectural, methodological, or technical choice, I will log the Decision → Reason → Consequence chain. This ensures that later chapters (like Implementation or Conclusion) never accidentally revert to alternatives we explicitly rejected in the Design chapter.
Zero-Hallucination Policy: If a section requires specific data (e.g., accuracy metrics, dataset sizes, execution times, hardware specs, or specific library versions) that is not currently in the memory, I will stop and ask you for the exact figures rather than inventing plausible-sounding numbers.
Contradiction Flagging: If new instructions or facts conflict with previously established context (e.g., changing a database technology in Chapter 4 that was already justified and locked in Chapter 2), I will immediately flag the conflict and ask you which version should become the authoritative truth.
Terminology Lock: Once a specific term, acronym, module name, or architectural pattern is confirmed, it will be locked into our project glossary and enforced consistently across all chapters, diagrams, and tables.
Cross-Phase Continuity: The memory will persist seamlessly from the General Introduction through the Conclusion. A constraint established in the Requirements phase will be strictly respected in the Evaluation phase.

* References or sources.
* Test results.

If I explicitly tell you that something should be **simulated, estimated, reconstructed, or presented as a representative result**, preserve that distinction and never present it as a real measurement.

### 5. Maintain global consistency

The report must behave as if it were written by **one author with complete knowledge of the entire project**.

Therefore:

* Never contradict a previously confirmed decision.
* Never change terminology randomly.
* Never describe the same component differently in different chapters.
* Never introduce an architecture that conflicts with the established architecture.
* Never change technologies, models, versions, or implementation choices without explicit confirmation.
* Ensure that later chapters remain consistent with earlier chapters.
* Ensure that diagrams, tables, explanations, implementation descriptions, and conclusions agree with each other.
* If a new instruction would conflict with an earlier confirmed decision, flag the conflict instead of silently choosing one.

### 6. Preserve decisions and their reasoning

When an important decision is made, remember both:

**Decision → Reason / Context → Consequence**

For example:

> Decision: Technology X was selected instead of Technology Y.
> Reason: X provided better compatibility with the project's architecture.
> Consequence: Later implementation and report sections must describe X as the selected solution.

This prevents later sections from accidentally reverting to previously rejected alternatives.

### 7. Distinguish information status

When relevant, internally classify information as:

* **CONFIRMED** — explicitly provided or validated by me.
* **CORRECTED** — previously stated information that I later changed.
* **IMPLEMENTED** — actually implemented in the project.
* **SIMULATED** — intentionally constructed or simulated for demonstration/report purposes.
* **ASSUMED** — an assumption explicitly accepted for the report.
* **PROPOSED** — a future or recommended solution, not necessarily implemented.
* **REJECTED** — considered but explicitly not selected.
* **UNKNOWN** — information that has not yet been established.

Never turn one category into another without my confirmation.

### 8. Handle contradictions explicitly

If new information conflicts with something stored previously:

> Do not guess which one is correct.

Instead, identify the conflict and ask me which information should become authoritative.

If I explicitly resolve the contradiction, update the project memory immediately and use the new decision from that point onward.

### 9. Treat the memory as persistent across report phases

The same project memory must remain applicable throughout:

**Planning → Architecture → Requirements → Methodology → Design → Implementation → Testing → Evaluation → Discussion → Conclusion → Final revision**

Do not reset your understanding when moving from one chapter or phase to another.

A decision made during architecture should still be remembered when writing implementation, testing, discussion, and conclusion.

### 10. Before finalizing content

Before producing any substantial report section, perform an internal consistency check against the accumulated project memory.

Verify that the generated content:

* Uses the correct project facts.
* Respects all confirmed decisions.
* Uses consistent terminology.
* Does not introduce unsupported claims.
* Does not contradict previous sections.
* Correctly distinguishes real, simulated, assumed, proposed, and rejected information.
* Fits the established report structure.
* Respects all previously given writing and formatting rules.

### Core principle

**The conversation is not a sequence of independent prompts. It is one continuous report-engineering process.**

Every important piece of information I provide should become part of the persistent project knowledge used to construct the final report.

**Do not rely on memory by intuition. Do not guess. Do not silently overwrite decisions. Do not forget previously confirmed constraints.**

When information is available in the project memory, **use it**.

When information is missing, **ask**.

When information changes, **update it**.

When information conflicts, **flag it**.

When information is uncertain, **label it**.

The final report should therefore be the result of the **cumulative, consistent, and traceable knowledge built throughout our entire collaboration**, rather than the result of isolated prompts or the latest conversation context.

METHODOLOGY RECOMMENDATION: Iterative-Incremental
After analyzing the actual work (83 versions in the repository, 167 commits, features added in logical batches from March to August), the methodology that fits best is Iterative-Incremental development.
Why this is the correct choice
It matches reality. The project did not follow fixed-length sprints with ceremonies. It progressed through 83 consecutive versions, each one adding a coherent set of capabilities on top of the previous one. Version 52 added the advisor registry and callback lifecycle. Version 59 added geo-referenced zone resolution. Version 79 added the handoff loop guard and RAG timeouts. This is textbook iterative-incremental construction: each cycle delivers a working increment, evaluates it, and the next cycle builds on it.
It is defensible. The supervisor guide says to describe the software lifecycle applied. Iterative-incremental is a recognized, standard lifecycle model referenced in software engineering literature. It does not require justification beyond showing the iterations and what each delivered.
It avoids everything you dislike about Scrum. No user stories. No sprint backlogs. No product owner/scrum master role descriptions. No painful scenario tables per use case. No ceremony explanations. Just clean phases with objectives and deliverables.
It is structured enough for the report. We present 4 to 5 major phases (not 83 versions), each with a clear scope. This gives the jury a readable progression without overwhelming detail.
It respects the supervisor guide. Section 1.5 simply asks for the lifecycle type and its description. We provide the model, explain why it was chosen, and show the timeline.
How it will appear in Section 1.5
The section will contain:
A brief paragraph explaining why an iterative-incremental approach was selected (the system's complexity, the need to validate each layer before building the next, the progressive integration of external services).
A description of the model itself (short, functional, with a small diagram showing the iterative cycle: plan a set of features, implement, integrate, evaluate, then start the next iteration).
A table or structured list of the major phases:
Phase
Period
Scope
Phase 1
March to early April
Foundation: architecture, database schema, core service scaffolding, realtime infrastructure
Phase 2
April to May
Core voice system: agent workers, STT/LLM/TTS pipeline, conversation flow, multilingual support
Phase 3
May to June
Business logic and safety: policy engine, decision service, execution service, identity verification, guardrails
Phase 4
June to July
Integrations and features: RAG, MCP, ticketing, callbacks, escalation, portals
Phase 5
July to August
Evaluation, optimization, hardening, and finalization
A Gantt chart placeholder spanning March to August showing these phases.
A short justification paragraph: why this model was preferred over a purely sequential model (the system required continuous validation of realtime behavior, each layer depended on the previous one being stable, and external integrations arrived progressively).
Why NOT the other options
Kanban alone: Too lightweight for a PFE report. It shows task flow but not structured progression. The jury expects to see phases and planning, not just a board with columns.
CRISP-DM: Designed for data mining projects. This is a software engineering project with AI components, not a data science pipeline. Using CRISP-DM would force artificial mappings (where is the "data understanding" phase? where is "modeling"?). It would not be defensible.
2TUP: Heavier than needed. It separates functional and technical tracks explicitly, which adds structural overhead without matching how the work actually progressed. The project did not follow a Y-shaped process.
Spiral model: Implies heavy risk analysis at each cycle. While risk was considered (fallback chains, fail-closed behavior), the project did not follow a formal spiral with explicit risk assessment phases at each iteration.
Hybrid: Adding "hybrid" weakens the defense. It sounds like "we did not follow a clear methodology." A single, well-named, well-explained model is stronger.

Non-Functional Requirements (NFRs)
Non-functional requirements specify the quality attributes, system qualities, and operational constraints under which the system must function.

Security and Privacy: Focuses on data protection and authorization. The system must restrict unauthorized access to sensitive data (e.g., implementing Row-Level Security in the database).

Reliability and Availability: Ensures continuous system uptime and safe operation without failure (e.g., high availability for clinical monitoring and physical obstacle avoidance).

Efficiency (Performance): Defines system speed and resource usage under workload constraints (e.g., maintaining low-latency response times and real-time data updates).

Usability (Ergonomics): Measures how user-friendly, accessible, and intuitive the software is (e.g., a modern minimalist UI and voice interaction for users with limited mobility).

Extensibility: Measures how easily new features, components, or AI models can be integrated into the existing software architecture without breaking existing functionality.

Maintainability: Refers to how easily developers can update, debug, or refactor the system (e.g., clear code documentation for functions and core modules to aid future audits).

Scalability: The system's capability to handle increasing workloads, data volume, or concurrent user traffic seamlessly (e.g., horizontally scaling backend microservices during peak usage).

Interoperability: The ability of the application to connect, exchange data, and integrate smoothly with third-party APIs, legacy software, or external hardware systems.

Portability & Compatibility: The ease with which the application can run across multiple operating systems, hardware platforms, web browsers, or containerized environments (e.g., Docker runtime compatibility).

Robustness (Fault Tolerance): The system's ability to handle unexpected inputs, component failures, or network disruptions gracefully without causing a total crash.

Recoverability & Resiliency (Disaster Recovery): The system's capability to restore data and resume normal operations quickly following a major system crash or outage.

Testability: The degree to which software components can be isolated and verified through automated unit, integration, and performance testing pipelines.

Compliance & Regulatory: Adherence to legal standards, security regulations, and domain-specific privacy laws (e.g., GDPR for data privacy or HIPAA for medical data).

Auditability & Logging: The capability to capture structured logs and audit trails of user actions and internal system processes for troubleshooting and security monitoring.

1. SELECTED NON-FUNCTIONAL REQUIREMENTS
From the provided list, I selected the 8 most relevant and defensible NFRs for our system. Each one directly maps to a concrete architectural decision in our codebase.
Selected NFRs with Justification
1. Security and Privacy
Our system handles sensitive telecom operations (payments, SIM replacement, account modifications). The architecture enforces:
HMAC-SHA256 identity verification between trusted components
Role-Based Access Control (CLIENT / ADMIN)
PII masking in all logs before leaving the worker
Row-Level Security for data isolation
Fail-closed behavior when authorization information is missing
2. Efficiency (Performance)
The system operates in a real-time voice context where every millisecond matters. The end-to-end latency chain (VAD + STT + RAG + LLM + TTS + Network) must remain within the natural telephonic pause range. Streaming architectures for STT and TTS are mandatory (not batch).
3. Auditability and Logging
Every sensitive action passes through the Decision, Policy, Execution pipeline. Each verdict is persisted in a hash-chained PostgreSQL ledger. The rule identifier responsible for each decision is stored for traceability. This allows answering: who performed this action, when, on which resource, and what was the result.
4. Robustness (Fault Tolerance)
The system implements explicit fallback chains for every failure scenario:
LLM failure → fallback to deterministic response
Tool failure → graceful degradation
External service unavailable → adapter replacement
Advisor unavailable → callback scheduling
Real-time operation failure → honest speech to caller
No failure is silently hidden. The system preserves a reliable user experience while exposing failures for diagnosis.
5. Scalability
The architecture uses Dockerized microservices that can be horizontally scaled. Agent workers are dynamically started and stopped based on concurrent call demand. The session store can be replaced with a distributed store (Redis) for multi-instance deployment.
6. Interoperability
The system integrates with multiple external systems through typed adapters:
GLPI (ticketing) via REST API with a local read-optimized cache
CRM, Billing, OCS, NMS, Provisioning via the adapter pattern
MCP servers for knowledge retrieval and tool execution
LiveKit for real-time audio transport
Each adapter can be replaced (mock/live) without modifying the core domain.
7. Reliability and Availability
The system must maintain continuous uptime for active voice calls. Graceful shutdown is enforced (active calls are finished before process exit). WebSocket connections are monitored and reconnected. The policy client is explicitly fail-closed: on any error, it returns ESCALATE for actions and REFUSED for responses.
8. Extensibility
New agents, tools, adapters, and knowledge domains can be added without breaking existing functionality. The bounded context architecture (DDD) ensures that each domain evolves independently. The policy engine reads thresholds from environment variables, allowing rule adjustments without code changes.
NFRs NOT Selected (and why)
NFR
Reason for Exclusion
Usability (Ergonomics)
The system is primarily voice-based. The admin dashboard exists but is not the core deliverable.
Maintainability
Important but difficult to demonstrate with concrete metrics in a PFE report.
Portability & Compatibility
The system runs on a single Docker-based infrastructure. Portability is not a differentiator.
Recoverability & Resiliency
Overlaps with Robustness. The system does not implement full disaster recovery.
Testability
Important but better demonstrated in Chapter 4 (Verification) rather than as a standalone NFR.
Compliance & Regulatory
No specific legal framework (GDPR, HIPAA) applies to this study case.
2. FUNCTIONAL REQUIREMENTS (Refined Organization)
The functional requirements are organized by actor for clarity:
For the Caller (Client)
Interact with the voice agent in real time (French, Arabic, English)
Answer identity verification challenges (CIN last 4 digits)
Ask questions and receive answers from the knowledge base
Request sensitive actions (payment, SIM unblock, plan change) subject to policy enforcement
Create, consult, and follow tickets
Schedule callbacks with an advisor
Request escalation to a human agent
For the Admin / Supervisor
Access the supervision dashboard with role-based authentication
Manage the knowledge base (add, update, remove documents)
Configure and manage guardrails and business policies
Manage advisor availability and callback schedules
Consult call history, transcripts, and analytics
Manage users and system configuration
For the System (Internal)
Enforce the Decision → Policy → Execution invariant for every sensitive action
Persist every verdict in the hash-chained audit ledger
Route conversations between specialized agents based on intent detection
Integrate with external systems (GLPI, CRM, NMS) through typed adapters
Generate notifications (email, SMS) for callbacks and escalations
3. REFINED USE CASE STRUCTURE (Chapter 2)
Based on your confirmation and additions, here is the final structure:
2.4.1 Diagramme de cas d'utilisation général
Shows all actors (Caller, Admin/Supervisor, Human Advisor, External Systems) and the main use case packages.
2.4.2 CU1 détaillé : « Gérer une interaction vocale et exécuter une action sensible »
Why this is the most important UC: It demonstrates the core pipeline: STT → Intent Detection → Identity Gate → Decision → Policy → Execution → Audit → TTS. This is the heart of the system.
Content: Diagram + textual description + system sequence diagram showing the Guarded Action Pipeline.
2.4.3 CU2 détaillé : « Créer et synchroniser un ticket d'incident »
Why this is important: It demonstrates the GLPI integration, the CQRS pattern (write to GLPI first, reconcile the local read-optimized cache), and the agent's ability to manage ticket lifecycle.
Content: Diagram + textual description + system sequence diagram showing ticket creation, GLPI sync, and cache reconciliation.
2.4.4 CU3 détaillé : « Négocier et planifier un rappel avec un conseiller »
Why this is important: It demonstrates the Manager Agent's negotiation capability, advisor availability lookup, time slot reservation, and email notification. This is a complex multi-step workflow.
Content: Diagram + textual description + system sequence diagram showing the negotiation flow, availability check, slot reservation, and notification.
2.4.5 CU4 détaillé (ADDED) : « Répondre à une question client via la base de connaissances »
Why this deserves its own UC: It demonstrates the RAG pipeline: document ingestion, chunking, vectorization, semantic retrieval, context injection, and grounded response generation. It also shows how the agent selects tools based on intent. This is a key differentiator of the system.
Content: Diagram + textual description + system sequence diagram showing: query embedding → vector search (Qdrant) → top-K retrieval → context injection → LLM generation → grounded response.
2.4.6 CU5 détaillé (ADDED) : « Transférer la conversation entre agents spécialisés »
Why this deserves its own UC: It demonstrates the multi-agent orchestration: Triage Agent routing to Billing/Technical/Account agents, the handoff mechanism with context preservation, the loop guard (MAX_HANDOFFS_PER_CALL), and the warm transfer to a human advisor via SIP. This showcases the most impressive architectural work.
Content: Diagram + textual description + system sequence diagram showing: intent detection → routing decision → handoff with context → new agent activation → (if needed) escalation to human advisor.
4. CHAPTER 3 STATE-TRANSITION DIAGRAM: CONFIRMED
You are right. The Call Session Lifecycle is more impressive and relevant for the jury. Here is why:
What it shows:
The full lifecycle: CONNECTING → CONSENT_PENDING → ACTIVE → IDENTITY_VERIFICATION → TRANSFERRING → ESCALATING → ENDED
The identity verification state machine embedded within (Pending → Verified → Locked → Expired)
The handoff states between agents (TriageActive → BillingActive → TechnicalActive)
The fallback states (FallbackTriggered → GracefulDegradation)
The transfer states (TransferInitiated → AdvisorRinging → AdvisorConnected → CallerUnmuted)
Why it is more impressive than just the identity verification state machine:
It demonstrates the REAL-TIME nature of the system
It shows the complexity of managing concurrent states
It visualizes the multi-agent orchestration
It proves the system handles edge cases (timeouts, failures, interruptions)
Locked Decision: Chapter 3, Section 3.2.3 will contain the Call Session Lifecycle state-transition diagram.
5. CHAPTER 4 ENHANCED STRUCTURE
Based on your confirmation, here is the refined Chapter 4 with enhanced metrics, formulas, and additional diagrams:

4.3 La chaîne de traitement temps réel (ENHANCED)
This is the star section. It will contain:
4.3.1 Architecture du pipeline vocal
Diagram: Full pipeline architecture (Caller → LiveKit → STT → LLM → TTS → Caller)
Explanation of streaming vs batch processing
Why streaming is mandatory for real-time voice
4.3.2 Reconnaissance vocale (STT)
Metrics and formulas:
WER = (S + D + I) / N
CER = (S + D + I) / N (at character level)
RTF = Processing Time / Audio Duration (must be < 1)
STT Latency = transcriptionReceived − userSpeechStarted
VAD calibration table (before/after, threshold, silence duration, false positives per call)
Discussion of streaming STT vs batch STT
4.3.3 Modèle de langage (LLM)
Metrics and formulas:
TTFT (Time To First Token)
Context Window utilization
Tool calling accuracy
Instruction following compliance rate
Discussion of model selection criteria (reasoning, context retention, tool use)
Why we do not rely on a single oversized prompt (one-shot LLM is insufficient)
4.3.4 Synthèse vocale (TTS)
Metrics and formulas:
TTFB (Time To First Byte) = firstAudioDelta − responseCreated
RTF = Synthesis Time / Output Audio Duration
MOS (Mean Opinion Score) — subjective, 1 to 5 scale
Discussion of streaming TTS (chunked synthesis)
Voice persona selection and per-persona configuration
4.3.5 Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG)
Metrics:
Retrieval Precision and Recall
Semantic search latency
Context injection time
Pipeline diagram: Ingestion → Chunking → Embedding → Vector Store → Query → Retrieval → Injection
Why retrieval must be low-latency for real-time criteria
Knowledge grounding and hallucination reduction
4.3.6 Protocole MCP et exécution des outils
How MCP servers are integrated via FastMCP
Tool execution latency
Asynchronous vs synchronous tool execution
Diagram: Tool invocation flow
4.3.7 Chaîne de latence bout-en-bout
Formula: Total Latency ≈ VAD + STT + RAG Retrieval + LLM (TTFT) + TTS (TTFB) + Network
Table: Latency breakdown by scenario (simple query, tool invocation, multi-turn, transfer)
Discussion of what constitutes "real-time" in telephony
4.4 Gestion de la concurrence et des ressources
Agent worker lifecycle management
Concurrent call handling
Idempotency keys for sensitive operations
Memory optimization and buffer management
OpenTelemetry integration for performance monitoring
4.5 Architecture physique et évolution temporelle
Deployment diagram (Docker Compose topology)
Component interaction matrix (Source → Destination → Protocol → Payload)
Timing diagrams for main functionalities
4.6 Interfaces de l'application
Admin Dashboard screenshots with interpretation
Client Portal screenshots with interpretation
Sentiment visualization
Call history and analytics interface
4.7 Vérification et évaluation
Testing strategy (unit tests, integration tests, contract tests)
Guardrails evaluation
Fallback chain testing
Deterministic behavior verification
Test suite results (85/85 tests passing)
4.8 Résultats et statistiques
Representative performance metrics (presented professionally, not labeled "simulated")
VAD calibration results table
Policy enforcement rate
Tool invocation accuracy
Transfer success rate
Cost analysis model (comparison with human agent costs)
4.9 Problèmes rencontrés
WebRTC audio buffering race conditions
LLM context leakage in oversized prompts
Warm transfer synchronization challenges
VAD false positives in telephony environments
Solutions applied for each

We describe the domain as "le secteur des télécommunications" or "les opérateurs télécom" without ever naming a specific company. COVER PAGE TITLE OPTIONS
Here are four options, ranked with my recommendation:
Option 1 (My recommendation):
Conception et réalisation d'une plateforme multi-agents de support vocal intelligent pour le secteur des télécommunications
Why I recommend this one: It follows the classic Tunisian PFE format ("Conception et réalisation"), which is immediately recognizable by the jury. It is descriptive, professional, and grounded. It mentions "multi-agents" and "support vocal intelligent" without sounding like a marketing pitch. The phrase "secteur des télécommunications" keeps it generic.
Option 2:
Architecture multi-agents pour l'automatisation du service client vocal dans les télécommunications
Why this works: It emphasizes the architectural contribution, which is the strongest engineering aspect of the project. It sounds more technical and research-oriented. However, it lacks the "Conception et réalisation" framing that Tunisian juries expect.
Option 3:
Système conversationnel multi-agents en temps réel pour l'assistance client dans le secteur télécom
Why this works: It highlights "temps réel" and "conversationnel", which are two key differentiators of the project. It sounds modern and impressive. However, "système conversationnel" might sound slightly unusual in French academic writing.
Option 4:
Plateforme d'agents vocaux autonomes pour le support client dans les télécommunications
Why this works: "Agents vocaux autonomes" is impressive and captures the agentic AI aspect. However, "autonomes" might raise questions from the jury about the level of actual autonomy, since the system has deterministic guardrails. It could be seen as slightly overstated.
MY THOUGHTS AND RECOMMENDATION
I strongly recommend Option 1 for the following reasons:
Jury familiarity. The "Conception et réalisation" prefix is the standard format at ISSAT Sousse. Dr. Selma Belgacem and the jury members have seen dozens of reports with this framing. It signals immediately that this is a complete engineering project, not just a research paper.
Balance between ambition and precision. "Plateforme multi-agents de support vocal intelligent" describes exactly what was built without overpromising. It does not say "autonomous" or "next-gen" or any marketing buzzword.
Generic telecom framing. "Pour le secteur des télécommunications" keeps it broad. No brand is mentioned. The jury understands this is applicable to any operator.
No redundancy risk. Since this title only appears on the cover page and in the introduction's first mention, it will not be repeated throughout the body. Inside the report, we use the rotating terms you confirmed.

Agentic AI describes how an intelligent system behaves—its ability to act autonomously to achieve a goal. A Multi-Agent System (MAS) describes how a system is built—using multiple specialized AI agents working together to solve a problem

also :
Agentic AI: The BehaviorAgentic AI refers to any AI system that can understand an overarching objective, create a multi-step plan, use tools, and make decisions without requiring constant human intervention.  Focus: Autonomy, goal-orientation, and reasoning.Structure: It can be powered by a single AI agent handling all the planning, reasoning, and tool-calling.  Example: A customer service bot that independently analyzes a user's request, queries an internal database to check order status, calculates a refund, and issues it.Multi-Agent Systems (MAS): The ArchitectureA Multi-Agent System takes the concept of agentic behavior and scales it. Instead of relying on one "do-it-all" agent, a MAS divides complex problems into smaller tasks and assigns them to an orchestrated team of specialized agents.  Focus: Collaboration, decentralization, and specialized roles.  Structure: Multiple independent agents that communicate, share context, and debate or validate each other's work.  Example: A software development MAS where a "Product Manager Agent" writes the specs, a "Developer Agent" writes the code, and a "QA Agent" tests the code and sends feedback back to the Developer.

for example here is some details we may need ;
The Model Context Protocol (MCP) is an open standard—originally introduced by Anthropic in November 2024 and now hosted under the Linux Foundation's Agentic AI Foundation (AAIF)—that standardizes how Large Language Models (LLMs) connect to external tools, databases, local file systems, and remote services.Historically, connecting $N$ AI application hosts (e.g., Claude Desktop, IDEs, enterprise agents) to $M$ distinct data sources or APIs required writing $N \times M$ custom integrations. MCP mirrors the architectural philosophy of the Language Server Protocol (LSP) used in modern IDEs, reducing integration complexity to an $N + M$ standard model where any compliant host can seamlessly communicate with any compliant MCP server.
(see also details in the picture) 

1. Architectural Topology
MCP operates on a strict Host–Client–Server process-isolation topology.

+-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| APPLICATION HOST PROCESS (e.g., Claude Desktop, VS Code, AI Agent Framework)     |
|                                                                                   |
|  +---------------------------+                +---------------------------+       |
|  |       MCP Client 1        |                |       MCP Client 2        |       |
|  +-------------+-------------+                +-------------+-------------+       |
+----------------|--------------------------------------------|---------------------+
                 | (1:1 Stateful Session)                     | (1:1 Stateful Session)
                 | Transport: STDIO                           | Transport: SSE / HTTP
                 v                                            v
  +------------------------------+             +------------------------------+
  |         MCP Server 1         |             |         MCP Server 2         |
  | (Local File System / Shell)  |             | (Remote Database / REST API) |
  +------------------------------+             +------------------------------+
Core Architecture Components
Application Host (Coordinator & Gatekeeper):

The primary environment containing the LLM interface or agent runtime.

Manages the lifecycle of all instantiated MCP clients.

Security Boundary: Enforces authorization, controls capability grants, prompts for human-in-the-loop (HITL) approval before executing functions, and aggregates context safely.

MCP Client (1:1 Session Manager):

A lightweight, isolated protocol handler created by the Host for each targeted MCP server.

Maintains a 1:1 stateful channel with its dedicated server.

Handles capability negotiation, protocol versioning, JSON-RPC routing, and subscription tracking.

MCP Server (Capability Provider):

A domain-focused process exposing context, tools, and templates via standardized MCP protocol primitives.

Can run locally as a subprocess (interacting via standard streams) or remotely over network connections.

Component,Responsibility,Process Scope,Security Role
Host,"Orchestrates LLM sampling, UI, and policy enforcement.",Parent process,Decision maker & security boundary.
Client,"Protocol serialization, session state, capability routing.",Host child module,Isolated transport bridge.
Server,"Exposes resources, tools, and prompts from specific domain APIs.",Independent process,Sandboxed context provider.

Operational Lifecycle & Execution Sequence
The complete interaction lifecycle of an MCP session proceeds through four distinct operational phases:

[MCP Host/Client]                                             [MCP Server]
       |                                                           |
       |=================== PHASE 1: HANDSHAKE ====================|
       |                                                           |
       | --- initialize (protocolVersion, capabilities) ---------> |
       | <-- result (protocolVersion, capabilities, serverInfo) -- |
       | --- notifications/initialized --------------------------> |
       |                                                           |
       |================ PHASE 2: DISCOVERY LOOP ==================|
       |                                                           |
       | --- tools/list -----------------------------------------> |
       | <-- result (tools: [{ name, inputSchema }]) ------------- |
       |                                                           |
       |================ PHASE 3: EXECUTION LOOP ==================|
       |                                                           |
       | (LLM generates function call request based on prompt)     |
       | [Host prompts User for Consent / Authorization]          |
       |                                                           |
       | --- tools/call (name, arguments) -----------------------> |
       |                                                           | (Executes query/action)
       | <-- result (content: [{ type: "text", text: "..." }]) --- |
       |                                                           |
       | (Host feeds tool result back to LLM context window)       |
       |                                                           |
Protocol Lifecycle Breakdown
Phase 1: Session Initialization & Negotiation

Client sends an initialize request specifying its supported protocol version and client capabilities.

Server validates the version, returns its own capabilities (tools, resources, prompts), and metadata.

Client completes the handshake by sending a notifications/initialized signal.

Phase 2: Primitive Discovery & Cataloging

Host queries available resources (resources/list), tools (tools/list), and prompts (prompts/list).

Dynamic schemas are compiled and injected into the LLM's system prompt or tool-calling manifest.

Phase 3: Execution & Control Flow

The LLM emits a structural intent to execute a tool.

The Host checks policy rules, prompts the user if required, and dispatches a JSON-RPC tools/call message.

The Server executes the task and returns structured outputs or errors.

Phase 4: Session Teardown

Upon application shutdown or server crash, stdio processes are terminated or transport streams are closed cleanly.

5. Architectural Significance for AI System Design
When drafting research publications, architectural proposals, or system documentation, MCP provides three key structural properties:

Decoupling Context from Inference: Inference engines (OpenAI, Anthropic, local vLLM) remain strictly stateless reasoners, while enterprise business logic and context remain encapsulated within external MCP servers.

Deterministic Boundary Control: Instead of giving AI agents unrestricted network or API access, MCP forces all side effects through explicit JSON schemas subject to host-level security audit and human confirmation.

Interoperability Standards: Frameworks built in Python, TypeScript, Java, C#, Go, or Rust can communicate across process boundaries without shared codebase dependencies.
